# WHAT THE NEXT FILTER DOES WITH IT: every distinct (producer, returned class,
# empty-or-not) `return-classes.txt` found, crossed with all 63 filters the
# engine binds.
#
# W2a. The defect this file exists for is not in any one filter's value -- it
# is in the TYPE flowing through the pipe, and no measurement of a filter in
# isolation can see it. `{{ m | keys | sort }}` throws on Kestra and answered
# here; so did seven other consumers over five producers.
#
# The producer list is GENERATED, not written: it is every
# (filter, returned class, length bucket) triple whose class in
# `return-classes.txt` is a collection or an array, taken at its first input in
# matrix order -- 46 of them. That rule is the whole point: a hand-written list
# is what produced "three", and then "five", when the measured consumer side is
# NINE. The bucket is 0, 1 or many, not empty-or-not, because
# `Collections.reverse` and `List.sort` disagree at exactly those three
# lengths.
#
# Format, tab separated:
#
#   producer  input  consumer  expr  kestra  egret  reason
#
# `input` names the same matrix as `return-classes.txt`. `kestra` and `egret`
# are the rendered text, or `error`, or -- in the `kestra` column only --
# `unstable`. Values are JSON-escaped.
#
# `expr` is the rendered expression, verbatim, and **the probe and answer maps
# are keyed by it**. They were keyed by an id built from the row's own three
# name columns, which meant a probe rewritten to `{{ 1 }}` -- with the row
# still reading `keys / m / sort` and the answers regenerated -- was fully
# self-consistent and green. `oracle/regex-classes.txt`, the precedent this
# file followed, keys its harness map on the probe's own context and does not
# have that hole; this column is how the same thing is done here.
#
# `unstable` is decided MECHANICALLY, not by eye: the probe corpus is rendered
# twice, once in file order and once REVERSED, and a cell whose answer moves is
# one whose value is a Java identity hash. **Forty-six cells move**, all of
# them `split` piped into a filter that stringifies its input; the count is
# pinned by `the_collection_pairs_match_the_engine`. (It was thirteen while the
# `Array` kind existed at one length only -- round 2's measured-length key
# added the empty and one-element arrays, and with them their `toString` rows.
# A few further rows of the same class do NOT move, only because their answer
# discards the hash; they carry their value and the same reason.) Note that two
# runs in file order
# agree perfectly -- the JVM's identity hash is a deterministic PRNG for a
# deterministic allocation sequence -- so re-running is NOT a sufficient
# instability test and the reversal is what earns this column.
#
# A row whose two columns AGREE carries `-`; every other row carries a reason.
# `runners::filters::the_collection_pairs_match_the_engine` asserts every
# egret cell by rendering it, and
# `..::the_collection_pair_fixtures_kestra_column_is_the_harnesss_own_answer`
# asserts the kestra column against `fixtures/collection-pairs.answers.json`.
#
# WHAT IT FOUND. 38 pairs were egret answering where Kestra throws --
# over-acceptance, which `oracle/README.md` says is the one divergence this
# crate does not allow, subject to the two recorded classes named in the
# clause beside that rule. (38 out of 2 142 when first measured; this file is
# 2 898 rows now, and the historical figure is deliberately not restated as a
# denominator.) The corpus held fourteen of them. Three
# CONSUMERS were missing from the recorded six:
#
#   `keys`        refuses a map's key view -- `{{ m | keys | keys }}` throws;
#   `escapeChar`  is `instanceof Map` -> `instanceof List` -> `toString()`, so
#                 a key view falls to the LAST branch: `{{ m | keys |
#                 escapeChar(type='shell') }}` is the text `[b, a]` there and
#                 was a walked list here. The fix round found this one, and it
#                 was hidden by a WRONG REASON: two rows were filed under the
#                 `java.util.HashMap` order even though both engines' orders in
#                 them are identical. A wrong reason does not merely mislead a
#                 reader, it closes the question;
#   `regexReplace` is really the HashMap-order class reaching the re-render
#                 loop, not a cast.
#
# All 38 are refusals now; see `runners::filters::JavaSeqKind`. A NINTH thing
# the kind decides is not a filter at all -- the subscript operator -- and is
# recorded in `JavaSeq::get_value`.
#
# The rest of the divergence is recorded, not fixed:
#   321  a Pebble-core name egret does not bind at all (`name-surface.txt`)
#    46  a Java array's `toString`, which is an identity hash
#    22  the `java.util.HashMap` iteration order `escapeChar` and `merge` impose
#    22  an internal Java class name, through `className`
#     1  the HashMap order reaching Kestra's re-render loop as `{#`
#
# **A depth-2 cross cannot see a propagator.** Every producer here is one
# filter applied to a context value, so nothing downstream of `reverse` or
# `sort` is measured, and that is exactly where this file's own worst bug hid.
# `propagation-pairs.txt` is the answer to that and must be read with it.
